# BCF Review, Fault-Class Math, and Whitepaper Content (2026-09-30)

This folder answers two requests in order. It sits next to `2026-09-30/`, which holds the earlier roadmap and master plan.

## Contents

| File                                                                                                             | Request | What it contains                                                                                                                                                                              |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| [01_BCF_Review_Integration_and_Whitepaper_Brainstorm.md](01_BCF_Review_Integration_and_Whitepaper_Brainstorm.md) | Task 1  | What was examined; 18 required corrections; 32 techniques with verdicts; master-plan integration (checklist J1-J16, experiments X1-X12); whitepaper v3 brainstorm against the official rubric |
| [02_BCF_Fault_Class_Math_Whitepaper_Content.md](02_BCF_Fault_Class_Math_Whitepaper_Content.md)                   | Task 2  | Named math phenomena; guesswork/information model; closed forms S(K, f) and K·f; masking/interference set-function model; chained-fault cost; pilot evidence; ready-to-edit v3 sections       |
| [03_Prior_Research_Fault_Interference_Masking.md](03_Prior_Research_Fault_Interference_Masking.md)               | Task 2  | Prior research on interference, masking, multi-fault diagnosis, multi-hunk repair and LLM agents; relevance map; how to identify interference-prone code                                      |
| [04_QC_Report.md](04_QC_Report.md)                                                                               | QC      | Markdown table QC results                                                                                                                                                                     |

## Five things to know first

| #   | Finding                                                                                                                                                                   | Evidence                               |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------- |
| 1   | Official paper rubric: Novelty, Quality (generality), Relevance, Verifiability, Clarity; each 0-5, averaged; **3,000-word limit**. Draft 2 is about 4,350 words           | Paper Track page, read live 2026-09-30 |
| 2   | Your claim is right in an idealized model: speed-up ≈ 1/(1/K + 1 − f), budgeted success × K·f. In general the limit is the information classes carry about fault location | Derived (doc 02 §B)                    |
| 3   | Issue text is a weak class signal: 0/129 tracebacks, 15/129 exception names; issue-text classes carried about 0.1 bits and made leave-one-out localization worse          | Measured (doc 02 §D)                   |
| 4   | Draft 2's graph protocol conflicts with the harness: symbol-name queries only, no `depth` or `called_by` parameters, only `calls` edges, no line numbers in nodes         | README + measured graphs (doc 01 §3)   |
| 5   | `httpx_6821` is invented and its symbols do not exist in the dataset's httpx snapshot; use it only as a labelled hypothetical                                             | Dossier ledger + measured (doc 02 §F)  |

## Sources and method

The competition docs (`input/kagglecomp/docs/markdown/`), `HARNESS_README.md`, the data zip, the `bcf/` folder, and the
competition website were used, including the Paper Track page linked from it. Scripts and outputs for every measured
number are in `input/kagglecomp/scratch/claude_work/t2/`.

The prior-research section (doc 03) is from background knowledge, because the instructions exclude outside sources.
Each citation carries a confidence rating and must be verified before use.

# Task 1 - BCF Material Review: Useful Techniques, Master-Plan Integration, Tests, and Whitepaper Brainstorm

**Prepared:** 2026-09-30
**Scope:** Examine the competition docs (`input/kagglecomp/docs/markdown/`) and the Batonic Coding Framework (BCF)
material (`bcf/BCF-Reference-Compendium.md`, `bcf/BCF-contestsubmission_dossier_20260928.html`). Then:

1. Highlight every useful area.
2. Show how each one would be incorporated into and tested within the existing master plan
   (`2026-09-30/02_Master_Plan_Day0_to_Final_Submission.md`).
3. Brainstorm the next whitepaper draft.

**Companion files:**

- `02_BCF_Fault_Class_Math_Whitepaper_Content.md`: Task 2, the math.
- `03_Prior_Research_Fault_Interference_Masking.md`: Task 2, prior research.

ID conventions follow the master plan: checklist items (A1-I5), failure modes (FM1-FM10), decision gates (DG1-DG6).
New items introduced here use **J1-J16** (checklist) and **X1-X12** (experiments).

---

## 1. What I Examined

| Source                                                                      | What it is                                                                                                                                            | New information vs. the existing plan                                                                                            |
| --------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `docs/markdown/Kaggle-00..04`                                               | Overview, Data, Getting Started notebook, Official Rules (snapshots of 2026-09-29)                                                                    | None. `Kaggle-00-Complete.md` is a concatenation of parts 01-04; I diffed it line by line and found no extra content.            |
| `bcf/BCF-Reference-Compendium.md`                                           | Specs for 8 in-submission items (A1-A8) and 6 dev-time items (B1-B6), with provenance labels                                                          | Rescue state machine, gates, spec schema, evidence tiers, ledger schema, verification hierarchy, benchmark notes                 |
| `bcf/BCF-contestsubmission_dossier_20260928.html`                           | 21-turn working session with 12 embedded artefacts, **two whitepaper drafts**, a provenance ledger, a primer and readiness sheet, a weeks 1-4 roadmap | Issue-class routing (Phase 1a), the crash-fork rule, taxonomy design, statistics plan, graph and embedding analyses, retractions |
| Paper Track page (linked from the competition URL; read live on 2026-09-30) | Official paper-track rules                                                                                                                            | **Official 5-criterion rubric, 3,000-word limit, prize definitions**. The dossier marks the rubric as never verified.            |

Two whitepaper drafts were extracted from the HTML's embedded frames: draft 1 is about 2,970 words without references;
draft 2 is about 4,350 words without references.

---

## 2. Headline Findings

1. **Keep the dossier's provenance discipline.** It retracts its own invented numbers (Table 1 of draft 1, the pass-rate
   chart, "zero wrong edits") and invented task (`httpx_6821`). Its evidence tags (Verified, Designed, Illustrative,
   Unsourced, Retracted) and the compendium's M/D/I/H claim tiers are the most valuable methodological assets in the
   material. They map directly onto the paper rubric's **Verifiability** criterion.
2. **Several technical statements in the drafts conflict with the official harness documentation and the dataset.**
   The graph protocol at the heart of draft 2 (natural-language dual queries, `called_by` edges, `depth`,
   `max_depth`, line numbers from node metadata) cannot run as written (§3).
3. **The official paper rubric is now known:** Novelty, Quality (generality beyond the competition), Relevance,
   Verifiability, Clarity. Each is scored 0-5 and the five are averaged. There is a **3,000-word maximum**. Draft 2 is about
   45% over the limit.
4. **Issue-class routing needs a different signal source.** My pilot on the 129 public tasks found **no Python tracebacks
   in any problem statement**, and only 15 of 129 name an exception type. The dossier's issue-text classifier labels 74%
   of tasks "unknown". Class-conditioned file priors *worsened* leave-one-out localization (details in document 02).
   Exception-class reasoning should run on failures the agent **produces itself** by reproduction, not on issue text.
5. **Novel, measurable paper material exists:** fault interference among the hunks of real reference patches
   ("interference atlas"), plus a search-theoretic account of when classification helps. It is novel, cheap to measure
   (CPU-only), and fits "Best New Resource" and "Best Paper".

---

## 4. Useful Techniques - Verdicts and Placement

Verdicts: **Adopt** (put in the plan as is), **Adapt** (change it to fit harness facts), **Test** (keep only if an
experiment shows value), **Park** (post-competition), **Reject**.

| ID  | Technique (source)                                                                                                                                         | Verdict                               | Why                                                                                                                 | Placement in master plan          |

| T2  | Spec-first Phase 1: hypothesis, target, fix shape, `depends_on` (A6; drafts §3.1)                                                                          | Test                                  | Plausible gain on multi-location tasks; costs output tokens under a tight time budget                               | X1 in S2 (DG2 window)             |
| T3  | Issue-class routing, Phase 1a (dossier Turn 20)                                                                                                            | Adapt                                 | Issue text carries little class signal (C12, pilot); classify the **observed failure signature** after reproduction | X2 in S2; math in doc 02          |
| T4  | Crash fork: state-fix vs. guard, "default to state-fix" (Turn 20)                                                                                          | Adopt                                 | Cheap prompt rule that counters symptom patching (overfitting patches); maps to FM3                                 | Prompt v1.x, S2                   |
| T5  | Feature-request spec shape: capability, integration point, precedent search (Turn 20)                                                                      | Adopt                                 | Feature tasks exist and the bug-fix spec does not fit them                                                          | Prompt branch, S2; labels from J4 |

## 5. Integration Into the Master Plan

### 5.1 New checklist section J (appended to doc 01 §10)

- [ ] J1 `hypotheses.md` committed with a date before each experiment (pre-registration); `deviations.md` for any later change (T19)
- [ ] J2 Receipts per run (receipt ID, git hash, config hash, seed, split) + evidence tags M/D/I/H on every paper number (T13)
- [ ] J3 Promptfoo suite: spec format, tool-call validity, class-router output on 10 frozen DEV issues (T15)
- [ ] J4 Taxonomy codebook v1 from open coding of DEV; fields: task_type, mechanism class, complexity tier, multi-location, root-cause file and symbol, fix shape, `depends_on` (T18)
- [ ] J5 Taxonomy labels for all 129 tasks; 20-task second-rater sample; Cohen's kappa (T18)
- [ ] J6 Skill `bcf_journal`: `/tmp/bcf/journal.md` writer/reader, repeat-action detector (T7)
- [ ] J7 Skill script `breaker.py`: strike counting, trip → rescue instructions (T6)
- [ ] J8 Crash-fork, feature-shape and contract-violation prompt branches (T3-T5)
- [ ] J9 Failure-signature classifier `sigclass.py`: parses repro/pytest output into exception class + innermost repo frame + assertion diff (T3 adapted; doc 02)
- [ ] J10 Interference-atlas runner: apply all subsets of reference-patch hunks, record per-test outcomes (doc 02 §E2)
- [ ] J11 Dataset analyses: graph structure, root-cause percentile, embedding within-vs-between with within-repo permutation null, symbol-query P@k (T20)
- [ ] J12 Rescue-with-fresh-context variant (T28)
- [ ] J13 V3 verifier sub-agent (advisory) + false-approval measurement (T9)
- [ ] J14 RFT compliance-filter arm (T24)
- [ ] J15 Paper v3 at or under 3,000 words, matched to the official rubric (§6)
- [ ] J16 Corrections C1-C18 applied to every reused text fragment

| S6     | Paper polish, 3,000-word check, submission by 11-10 (2 days of buffer before 11-12)                                         | J15                      |

### 5.3 Experiment cards

Common rules for all cards: paired design on the same tasks; primary split DEV (99 tasks) or DEV-FAST (24) as stated;
confirmation on LOCKBOX only at gates; exact McNemar for resolve counts, Wilcoxon signed-rank + paired bootstrap CI for
continuous metrics, Holm correction across a card's family; seed-2 noise floor reported.

| ID  | Question                                                             | Arms                                                                              | Primary metric                                                        | Decision rule                                                                                         | Sprint |
| --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------ |
| X1  | Does a Phase 1 spec help under a 4-minute budget?                    | (a) no spec; (b) 5-line text spec; (c) JSON spec                                  | Resolved tasks; hit@file; generated tokens                            | Keep (b) or (c) only if +3 tasks on DEV and tokens per task rise by 15% or less                       | S2     |
| X2  | Does classification help localization, and which signal?             | (a) none; (b) issue-text class; (c) failure-signature class after repro; (d) both | Expected guesses to hit the fix file (LOO); calls before first edit   | Keep a routing arm only if it beats (a) on paired calls-before-edit with p < 0.05 and no resolve loss | S2     |
| X3  | Do circuit breakers + journal cut loops and recover failures?        | (a) none; (b) journal only; (c) journal + breaker; (d) (c) + fresh-context rescue | Loop rate; repeat-action rate; rescue recovery rate; resolved         | Keep the cheapest arm within noise of the best                                                        | S2-S3  |
| X4  | Hand-off format for scout → fixer                                    | T0 full transcript; T1 structured packet; T2 packet + last N tool outputs         | Resolved; tokens per task                                             | Prefer T1 if within noise of T0 at 30% or fewer tokens                                                | S2     |
| X5  | Does a separate verifier sub-agent add value?                        | (a) none; (b) advisory V3 before submit                                           | False-approval rate (V3 OK, hidden tests fail); resolved              | Keep only if resolved rises; never allow V3 to override failing tests                                 | S4     |
| X6  | Do root-cause symbols sit at graph hubs?                             | Descriptive                                                                       | Percentile of the root-cause node by degree and betweenness, per repo | Report as is (either answer is publishable)                                                           | S3     |
| X7  | Does `search_similar_code` retrieve root causes from symbol queries? | Query = identifiers extracted from the issue vs. random-symbol control            | P@1/3/10 of the root-cause symbol                                     | If P@10 is below 20%, demote graph search behind `locate.py` in prompts                               | S3     |
| X8  | Does a generic fix-pattern memory transfer to unseen repos?          | (a) none; (b) pattern resource via `load_skill_resource`                          | Resolved on PSEUDO-PRIVATE                                            | Keep only on a PSEUDO-PRIVATE gain                                                                    | S4     |
| X9  | Interference atlas (doc 02 §E2)                                      | All hunk subsets of reference patches                                             | Prevalence of additive / conjunctive / masking / antagonistic tasks   | Descriptive with Wilson CIs                                                                           | S3     |
| X10 | Optimal number of classes K                                          | K in {1, 2, 4, 8, 12} with nested CV                                              | Measured fidelity f(K) and guesswork reduction                        | Compare with the theory in doc 02 §B                                                                  | S3-S4  |
| X11 | Misattribution rate q on multi-location tasks                        | Baseline vs. BCF-FC                                                               | q, recovery cost r, fix-order errors                                  | Test the cost model of doc 02 §C3                                                                     | S4     |
| X12 | Compliance-filtered RFT                                              | Shortest-successful vs. shortest-successful + protocol-compliant                  | Resolved; tokens per task                                             | Keep the filter only on a paired DEV gain                                                             | S4     |

## 6. Brainstorm - Next Whitepaper Draft (v3)

# Task 2 - The Mathematics of Fault-Class Search in BCF: Classification, Masking, Interference and Chained Issues

**Prepared:** 2026-09-30
**Purpose:** Put rigorous math behind the BCF claim below, name the mathematical phenomena it invokes, test it against
real data from the public training set, and supply ready-to-edit content for whitepaper draft v3.

> *"I aim to build BCF to help solve more issues sooner by classifying errors into exception classes. This effectively
> limits the search space to the most probable root causes. ... Therefore, the speed and overall effectiveness of the
> algorithm will simply depend on the adjustments to the number and fidelity of the exception categories."*

**Evidence tags** (from the BCF compendium B1): **M** = measured here, with the script named; **D** = derived by
stated arithmetic; **I** = illustrative, not data; **H** = hypothesis. Scripts and outputs are in
`input/kagglecomp/scratch/claude_work/t2/`: `pilot_stats.py`, `pilot_perm.py`, `graph_stats.py`, and their `.json` outputs.

**One-paragraph verdict.** The statement is **substantially right in an idealized model, and incomplete in practice.**
The idealized result is exact and simple: with K equally sized classes and classifier fidelity f, unbounded search gets
faster by 1/(1/K + 1 − f). Under a tight budget, the success rate multiplies by K·f. So "number and fidelity" really are
the two knobs. In general, though, the quantity that governs speed is the **information the classes carry about the
fault location**, I(L; Ĉ), which requires more than number and fidelity: it also needs **alignment** between classes and
code locations, **enough data** to estimate class-conditional priors, and a **routing policy that reorders rather than
prunes**. My pilot on the 129 public tasks shows why this matters. Issue-text classes carry about 0.1 bits about the fix
file, and using them as priors **hurt** leave-one-out localization. Exception classes become valuable when they are
taken from **failures the agent provokes itself** (reproduction). Fault masking, interference and chained issues need a
second mechanism: **re-probing after each fix and ordering fixes along a fault dependency graph**.

---

## A. Which Mathematical Phenomena Your Statement Describes

| Phrase in the statement                                    | Named phenomenon                                                                             | Field                                  | What it contributes                                                                                    |
| ---------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| "classifying errors into exception classes"                | Conditioning on evidence; a classifier as a **noisy channel**                                | Bayesian inference; information theory | Class = observation that updates a prior over locations                                                |
| "limits the search space to the most probable root causes" | **Bayesian search theory**; optimal ordering by probability per unit cost                    | Operations research                    | Inspect candidates in decreasing posterior-per-cost order                                              |
| "solve issues sooner"                                      | **Guesswork** (expected number of guesses) and its entropy bounds (Massey 1994; Arikan 1996) | Information theory                     | Expected search effort grows **exponentially with entropy**: each bit of information roughly halves it |
| "solve *more* issues" under a time limit                   | **Budgeted search / coverage probability**                                                   | Search theory                          | Success = posterior mass inside the first B inspections                                                |
| "number of categories"                                     | Capacity bound I ≤ log2 K; **bias-variance** of estimating K class priors from n tasks       | Information theory; statistics         | More classes can carry more bits, but need more data                                                   |
| "fidelity of categories"                                   | **Data-processing inequality** and **Fano's inequality**                                     | Information theory                     | Misclassification destroys information; fidelity caps the achievable speed-up                          |
| Repeated classification into sub-classes                   | **Twenty questions / Huffman coding**; with errors, **Ulam's searching-with-lies game**      | Coding theory; combinatorial search    | Expected yes/no questions is at least H(L); noisy answers need redundancy                              |
| "fault masking"                                            | **Reachability / propagation failure** in the RIPR (PIE) fault model                         | Software testing theory                | One fault blocks another fault's execution or its effect on outputs                                    |
| "fault interference"                                       | **Non-additive interaction (epistasis)** of faults or fixes on the test-outcome function     | Set functions; Möbius inversion        | Measurable interaction indices over subsets of fixes                                                   |
| "multi-step, chained issues"                               | **Sequential diagnosis** on a fault **dependency DAG** under partial observability           | Model-based diagnosis; POMDPs          | Fix order = topological order; re-probing reveals masked faults                                        |
| "turns lost fixing symptoms first"                         | **Geometric retry cost** under misattribution probability q                                  | Probability                            | Expected waste per chain link = q/(1 − q) attempts                                                     |

---

## B. Formal Model: Classification-Guided Localization

### B1. Setup

A task's repository offers a candidate set Θ of code locations (functions, methods, classes). In the released graphs,
the candidate pools are large:

| Repo (task graph measured) | Nodes | `calls` edges | Nodes in main package | Isolated nodes | Tag |
| -------------------------- | ----- | ------------- | --------------------- | -------------- | --- |
| fastapi (`fastapi_15661`)  | 4,263 | 2,430         | 344                   | 3,061          | M   |
| rich (`rich_4070`)         | 2,015 | 5,762         | 1,068                 | 70             | M   |
| requests (`requests_7505`) | 759   | 1,884         | 299                   | 60             | M   |
| httpx (`httpx_3672`)       | 740   | 1,618         | 357                   | 21             | M   |

Let L ∈ Θ be the (unknown) root-cause location with prior π. The agent inspects candidates one at a time at cost c per
inspection (a read, a test run or a graph call). It observes evidence X (issue text, reproduction output) and maps it to
a predicted class $\hat{C} = g(X) \in \{1,\dots,K\}$.

### B2. Search cost is guesswork, and guesswork is exponential in entropy

Inspecting in decreasing order of probability, $p_{(1)} \ge p_{(2)} \ge \dots$, is optimal for unit costs. With unequal
costs, the rule is decreasing $p_i / c_i$ (with detection probabilities $d_i$: decreasing $p_i d_i / c_i$). The expected
number of inspections is the **guesswork**

$$G(\pi)  =  \sum_{i\ge 1} i   p_{(i)} .$$

Two classic bounds tie guesswork to entropy. Arikan (1996, ρ = 1):

$$\frac{\big(\sum_i \sqrt{p_i}\big)^2}{1+\ln |\Theta|}  \le  G(\pi)  \le  \Big(\sum_i \sqrt{p_i}\Big)^2  =  2^{H_{1/2}(\pi)} ,$$

where $H_{1/2}$ is the Rényi entropy of order ½. Massey (1994): $G \ge 2^{H(\pi)-2}+1$ whenever $H(\pi) \ge 2$ bits.

**Consequence:** up to a logarithmic factor, **search effort is exponential in the uncertainty about the location**.
Every bit of information the agent acquires about L roughly halves the expected number of inspections.

### B3. Classification helps on average, if the posteriors are right

Let $G^*(L \mid \hat{C})=\sum_{\hat c} P(\hat c)  G\big(\pi(\cdot \mid \hat c)\big)$ with each conditional ordering optimal.
Then

$$G^*(L \mid \hat{C})  \le  G^*(L).$$

*Proof sketch:* for every ĉ, the unconditional ordering is a feasible ordering; averaged over ĉ its cost equals $G^*(L)$,
so the best conditional ordering cannot be worse. ∎

The inequality needs the **true** conditional distributions. With **estimated** conditionals $\hat{\pi}(\cdot \mid \hat c)$,
the conditional ordering can be worse than the unconditional one. This is exactly what the pilot (§D) observes.

### B4. The information budget: number and fidelity

The benefit is bounded by the mutual information between location and predicted class. If the classifier sees the
location only through the true class (Markov chain $L \to C \to \hat{C}$):

$$I(L;\hat{C})  \le  \min\{  I(L;C),  I(C;\hat{C})  \}  \le  H(\hat C)  \le  \log_2 K \quad \text{(data-processing inequality)}$$

and, with misclassification probability $P_e$ (Fano):

$$I(C;\hat{C})  \ge  H(C) - h(P_e) - P_e \log_2 (K-1).$$

So **number** sets the ceiling ($\log_2 K$ bits), **fidelity** determines how much of it survives, and **alignment**
($I(L;C)$: do classes actually correspond to different code regions?) determines whether there was anything to gain.
Equal-sized classes that are unrelated to where bugs live give $I(L;C)=0$ at any fidelity.

### B5. A closed form: speed-up as a function of K and f

**Idealized model (uniform-in-class).** Θ has N candidates split into K regions of m = N/K. L is uniform within its
true region. The classifier is correct with probability f, and otherwise names one of the other K − 1 regions uniformly.
The Bayes-optimal order (for f ≥ 1/K) searches the predicted region first, then the rest.

$$E[G_{\text{none}}]=\frac{N+1}{2}, \qquad
E[G_{\text{class}}]=f\cdot\frac{m+1}{2} + (1-f)\Big(m+\frac{N-m+1}{2}\Big)=\frac{m+1+(1-f)N}{2}.$$

**Result 1 (unbounded search).**

$$S(K,f)=\frac{E[G_{\text{none}}]}{E[G_{\text{class}}]}  \approx  \frac{1}{ 1/K + (1-f) } \quad (N \gg K).$$

- With perfect fidelity the speed-up equals the number of classes: S = K.
- With imperfect fidelity the speed-up is capped by **1/(1 − f) regardless of K**. At f = 0.8, no number of classes
  gives more than 5×.
- Classification helps iff f > 1/K (better than chance).

**Result 2 (budget-limited search, the competition regime).** With a budget of B ≤ m inspections:

$$P_{\text{none}}(\text{found})=\frac{B}{N}, \qquad P_{\text{class}}(\text{found})=f\cdot\frac{B}{m}=f K \cdot \frac{B}{N}.$$

The success multiplier is **K·f**, which is literally "number × fidelity". This is the precise sense in which the
statement is correct.

**Result 3 (when to split a category).** Refining one partition level from K to K′ > K, with fidelity dropping from f
to f′, increases S iff

$$\frac{1}{K}-\frac{1}{K'}  >  f-f' .$$

Example: splitting 4 classes into 8 gains 0.125, so it pays only if fidelity falls by less than 12.5 percentage points.

**Result 4 (never prune).** Hard pruning (searching only the predicted region) caps the success probability at f even with
unlimited budget. Reordering (search predicted region first, then the rest) keeps the speed-up and never loses coverage.
**Design rule: classes reorder candidates; they never delete them.**

Speed-up table from Result 1 (tag **D**):

| K (classes)    | f = 0.6 | f = 0.8 | f = 0.9 | f = 1.0   |
| -------------- | ------- | ------- | ------- | --------- |
| 2              | 1.11x   | 1.43x   | 1.67x   | 2.00x     |
| 4              | 1.54x   | 2.22x   | 2.86x   | 4.00x     |
| 8              | 1.90x   | 3.08x   | 4.44x   | 8.00x     |
| 16             | 2.16x   | 3.81x   | 6.15x   | 16.0x     |
| cap as K grows | 2.50x   | 5.00x   | 10.0x   | unbounded |

Reading the table: at K = 4, raising fidelity from 0.8 to 0.9 (2.22x → 2.86x) is worth almost as much as doubling
K at constant fidelity (→ 3.08x). Finer classes usually lose fidelity. Doubling K from 4 to 8 pays only if fidelity falls
by less than 12.5 points (Result 3). For example, K = 8 at f = 0.7 gives only 2.35x.

### B6. The small-data problem ("number" has a hidden cost)

Class-conditional priors are estimated from n training tasks. The plug-in estimate of $I(L;C)$ is biased upward by
approximately

$$\text{bias}  \approx  \frac{(\lvert\mathcal{L}\rvert-1)(K-1)}{2n\ln 2}\text{ bits},$$

where $\lvert\mathcal{L}\rvert$ is the number of distinct locations. For fastapi (n = 67, 28 distinct fix files, K = 5)
this is about 1.2 bits (**D**). That is the same order as the permutation-null mean I measured (0.78 bits, §D). More
classes multiply the number of cells to estimate. With about 100 tasks, **file-level** class priors are not estimable,
but **mechanism-level strategies** (which way to search, what to look for) can still be learned and transfer to unseen
repositories.

### B7. Classification as an active probe (where exception classes really live)

The agent can **buy** evidence by running a reproduction or an existing test at cost $c_r$. The output is a failure
signature Y: exception type in the Python hierarchy, innermost repository frame, assertion diff. The expected value of
that information, in inspections saved, is

$$\text{EVI}  =  G^*(L) - E_Y\big[G^*(L \mid Y)\big]  -  c_r / c .$$

Probe iff EVI > 0. This is the minimum-entropy probing principle of model-based diagnosis. The Python exception
hierarchy (for example `LookupError` ⊃ `KeyError`, `IndexError`) also gives a **natural coarse-to-fine class tree**.
Descend the tree only while Result 3 says the finer split pays. This turns "number of categories" into an adaptive
choice per task instead of a global constant.

---

## C. Fault Masking, Fault Interference and Chained Issues

### C1. Test outcomes as a set function

Let Φ be the set of faults present and T* the hidden tests. For each test t, the failure indicator is $F_t(\Phi)\in\{0,1\}$.
The task is resolved iff $F_t(\Phi_{\text{final}})=0$ for all t ∈ T*.

A fault f makes t fail only if all four **RIPR** conditions hold: t **R**eaches f, f **I**nfects the program state, the
infection **P**ropagates to an observed point, and an assertion **R**eveals it.

For two faults a and b:

| Relation                  | Definition on test t                                                                                                | Intuition                                               |
| ------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- |
| Independent (additive)    | $F_t(\{a,b\}) = F_t(\{a\}) \lor F_t(\{b\})$                                                                         | Each fault fails the tests it would fail alone          |
| **Masking** of b by a     | $F_t(\{b\})=1$ but the failure signature of t under {a, b} is a's alone; strong form: b unreachable while a present | a blocks b's Reachability or Propagation                |
| Synergy                   | $F_t(\{a,b\})=1$ while $F_t(\{a\})=F_t(\{b\})=0$                                                                    | Failure only from the combination                       |
| Cancellation (antagonism) | $F_t(\{a\})=1$ or $F_t(\{b\})=1$, but $F_t(\{a,b\})=0$                                                              | "Two wrongs make a right"; fixing one exposes a failure |

**Interference** is any departure from the additive row. Masking and cancellation are why "fix one, break one" happens.

### C2. Measuring interference on fixes: the Möbius interaction index

We cannot remove faults one by one in real tasks, but we **can** apply subsets of the reference fix. Split a reference
patch into units U = {u1, …, uk} (hunks or files). Let $P_t(S)\in\{0,1\}$ record whether test t passes with units S ⊆ U
applied. The Möbius (Harsanyi) transform

$$\Delta_t(S)  =  \sum_{T \subseteq S} (-1)^{\lvert S\rvert - \lvert T\rvert}  P_t(T)$$

measures the **pure interaction** of the units in S. All $\Delta_t(S)$ with $\lvert S\rvert \ge 2$ vanish iff the fix
is additive. This is the same algebra used for **epistasis** in genetics and interaction indices in explainable ML. For
two units with a fail-to-pass test ($P_t(\varnothing)=0$) and a complete patch that passes ($P_t(\{i,j\})=1$), the three
possible truth tables are:

| P(ui) | P(uj) | P(ui, uj) | Δ(i,j) | Name                              | Meaning for an agent                                                               |
| ----- | ----- | --------- | ------ | --------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 1     | 0     | 1         | 0      | Single-unit sufficient (additive) | One hunk carries the fix; the other is cleanup or a secondary improvement          |
| 1     | 1     | 1         | −1     | Redundant (substitutes)           | Either change alone fixes the test; the agent may stop early                       |
| 0     | 0     | 1         | +1     | **Conjunctive / chained**         | Neither change shows progress alone; progress is **masked** until both are present |

With k ≥ 3 units, and for pass-to-pass tests, **antagonistic** patterns also appear: a partial patch that breaks a test
the full patch keeps green. That is the "wrong-order fix causes new failures" behaviour the BCF drafts describe.

### C3. Chained issues as sequential diagnosis on a dependency DAG

Model the faults of a task as a DAG D. An edge $f_i \to f_j$ means $f_j$ is masked, or its symptom is produced, until
$f_i$ is fixed. The agent sees only the current failure signatures.

- **Correct order** = a **topological order** of D. Fixing a downstream fault first either has no visible effect
  (masked) or suppresses the symptom (a guard at the symptom site). The suppression case produces a **plausible but
  incorrect** patch: visible tests pass and hidden tests fail.
- **Misattribution cost.** Suppose each attempt to fix the next link picks the wrong site (a downstream symptom) with
  probability q. Each attempt costs c, and each wrong attempt adds recovery r (revert + re-diagnose). The attempts per
  link are geometric, so for a chain of k links

$$E[\text{cost}]  =  k c  +  (c+r)\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{q_j}{1-q_j}.$$

- **When is the advantage "superlinear"?** A method that lowers $q_j$ to $q'_j$ saves
  $(c+r)\sum_j \big[q_j/(1-q_j) - q'_j/(1-q'_j)\big]$. The saving is linear in k if q is constant. It is
  **superlinear only if misattribution grows with chain depth** (deeper faults produce more misleading symptoms). This
  turns draft 1's "superlinear advantage" claim into a **testable condition** (experiment X11).
- **Stopping rule for masked faults.** After a fix, the probability p that another masked fault remains is updated by a
  clean re-run with sensitivity s (the chance the re-run would reveal it):
  $p' = p(1-s)/\big(p(1-s)+1-p\big)$. BCF's re-probe step is the operational form: **after every fix, re-run the
  reproduction and the whole affected test file; a new failure signature is an unmasking event, so re-classify and
  continue on the DAG.**

### C4. The BCF-FC loop (fault-class search)

```text
prior    <- locate.py (lexical + structural ranking; repo-agnostic)       # B1-B2
repeat until budget checkpoint:
    Y    <- probe(reproduction or closest existing test)                   # B7: buy evidence
    c    <- sigclass(Y)  # exception type / innermost repo frame / assertion kind
    rank <- reorder(prior, posterior(c))        # B5 Result 4: reorder, never prune
    pick strategy by class:
        invalid-state crash -> walk callers / upstream producers first (fix the state, not the crash site)
        guard-missing crash -> crash site
        silent wrong output -> producer of the wrong value (callees of the symptom site)
        feature             -> nearest existing precedent
    fix top hypothesis with the smallest edit
    Y'   <- re-probe + run the whole affected test file                    # C3 stopping rule
    if new signature: journal "unmasked", continue (next DAG link)
    elif same signature: strike; after 3 strikes revert and take the next hypothesis
    else: done -> guard -> submit
```

---

## D. Pilot Evidence From the Public Training Set (already measured)

All from `tasks.jsonl` (129 tasks; fastapi 67, rich 48, requests 13, httpx 1). These are dataset statistics, not agent
runs.

| Measurement                                                                                     | Value                                                        | Tag | Script            |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ | --- | ----------------- |
| Problem statements containing a Python traceback                                                | **0 / 129**                                                  | M   | `pilot_stats.py`  |
| Problem statements naming an exception type (for example `AssertionError`, `TypeError`)         | 15 / 129 (11.6%)                                             | M   | `pilot_stats.py`  |
| Test patches asserting an exception (`pytest.raises`)                                           | 15 / 129                                                     | M   | `pilot_stats.py`  |
| Dossier issue-text classifier (Appendix C) output                                               | unknown 95, logic 16, crash 9, feature 6, edge_case 3        | M   | `pilot_stats.py`  |
| Leave-one-out file guesswork, repo-only prior (88 tasks whose fix file appears in another task) | mean rank 5.84, median 3, top-1 30, top-3 52                 | M   | `pilot_stats.py`  |
| Same, repo + class-conditioned prior                                                            | mean rank **6.57**, median 4, top-1 **23**, top-3 41 (worse) | M   | `pilot_stats.py`  |
| Tasks whose fix file never appears in another task of the same repo                             | 41 / 129 (32%)                                               | M   | `pilot_stats.py`  |
| Plug-in I(fix file; class), fastapi: observed vs. permutation null mean (2,000 shuffles)        | 0.90 vs. 0.78 bits; excess about 0.12 bits; p = 0.076        | M   | `pilot_perm.py`   |
| Same, rich                                                                                      | 0.52 vs. 0.46 bits; p = 0.19                                 | M   | `pilot_perm.py`   |
| Same, requests                                                                                  | 0.37 vs. 0.39 bits; p = 0.68                                 | M   | `pilot_perm.py`   |
| Reference patches with more than one hunk / more than one source file                           | 71 / 129 (55%) / 38 / 129 (29%)                              | M   | `pilot_stats.py`  |
| Tasks with 2-4 source hunks (full subset enumeration feasible)                                  | 36                                                           | M   | `hunk_stats.json` |

**Interpretation (tag D/H).**

1. Issue text is a weak channel: it almost never carries a traceback, so "exception classes" cannot come from it.
2. Coarse text classes carry about 0.1 bits about the fix file. Under B2 that is at most about a 7% reduction in search
   effort, and estimation noise more than cancels it (the leave-one-out result).
3. Location priors do not transfer well even within a repo (32% of fix files are unseen), let alone to the private
   test repos.

Together these support the design shift in B7: **classify the failure the agent reproduces, and map classes to search
strategies, not to file priors.**

---

## E. Measurement Plan That Turns the Math Into Results

| ID  | Experiment                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Output for the paper                                                       | Cost and machine                                                             |
| --- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| E1  | Pilot facts (§D)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Table T1                                                                   | Done                                                                         |
| E2  | **Interference atlas:** for the 36 tasks with 2-4 source hunks, apply all 2^k hunk subsets in Container B with `test_patch`; record every test's outcome; compute Δ and classify tasks as additive / redundant / conjunctive / antagonistic. Extend to the 38 multi-file tasks by grouping hunks per file | Figure F3 with Wilson CIs; released CSV (instance id + unit id + outcomes) | About 340-800 verification runs; CPU-only; about 1-3 h on AUX2 at 6 parallel |
| E3  | Fidelity vs. K: human mechanism labels (taxonomy J4-J5); classifiers on (a) issue text and (b) reproduction signature; nested CV for K ∈ {1, 2, 4, 8, 12}                                                                                                                                                 | Measured (K, f) points overlaid on the S(K, f) surface (F2)                | Labeling about 20 h; CPU                                                     |
| E4  | Signature classification in the agent: X2 arms (none / issue-text / reproduction signature / both)                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Calls before first edit; hit@file; resolved                                | DEV runs on MAIN                                                             |
| E5  | Cost-model validation on the E2 conjunctive tasks: estimate q, c, r from traces; compare predicted vs. observed cost; baseline vs. BCF-FC                                                                                                                                                                 | Test of the C3 formula and the superlinearity condition                    | Traces from E4                                                               |
| E6  | Delta-debugging cross-check: ddmin over hunks to find minimal fixing subsets                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | Validates E2 on large patches (k > 4) without 2^k runs                     | CPU                                                                          |

Pitfalls and controls:

- Some hunk subsets will not import or apply. Record them as their own outcome class, and group hunks by function to
  reduce invalid combinations.
- Use exact McNemar and paired bootstrap for agent comparisons.
- Use a within-repo permutation null for every mutual-information claim.
- State that all measurements are on the public repos, which differ from the private test repos.

---

## F. Worked Example: the Hypothetical "httpx_6821" (tag I)

> **Status:** `httpx_6821` is **not** a competition task (the dossier's ledger records that it was invented). Its symbols
> (`_send_handling_redirects`, `Response.stream`) do **not exist** in the dataset's httpx snapshot, which uses
> `RedirectMiddleware` and `NetworkStream` (measured, `httpx_symbol_check.py`). Use it only as a labelled
> hypothetical, and prefer a real conjunctive task from E2 in the paper.

**Faults.**

- a: redirect history reset on a scheme change (state-accumulation fault).
- b: a hard `assert` on a consumed stream (guard-missing fault).

**DAG.** a → b: b is reachable only when history is preserved. That is reachability masking.

**Two readings of the scenario.** The dossier's text is ambiguous; the issue says b "only surfaces after" a is fixed,
while the naive trace sees b's `AssertionError` first. The math separates the two cases:

| Reading                     | What the agent observes first                                       | Class of first signature                  | Correct action                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Masking (b invisible)       | `len(history) == 1` instead of 3 (wrong value, no exception)        | Silent wrong output → state accumulation  | Find the producer of `history` (a); fix; **re-probe** → new signature `AssertionError` in library code = unmasking → crash fork says guard-missing (consumed stream is a legitimate state) → fix b |
| Interference (both visible) | `AssertionError` inside library code **and** a wrong history length | Two signatures; the crash is more salient | Order by the DAG: a wrong **value** upstream of a later **crash** puts the value fault first; the crash fork's default "fix the state, not the crash site" prevents guarding b first               |

**Cost model with assumed parameters** (tag I; parameters are assumptions for illustration, to be measured in E5):
k = 2 links, c = 3 tool calls per attempt, r = 2 recovery calls.

| Agent                                         | Misattribution q  | Expected cost $2c + (c+r)\sum_j q_j/(1-q_j)$ |
| --------------------------------------------- | ----------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| Symptom-first (fixes the salient crash first) | q = 0.5 per link  | 6 + 5 × (1 + 1) = **16 calls**               |
| BCF-FC (probe, classify, DAG order, re-probe) | q′ = 0.1 per link | 6 + 5 × (0.111 + 0.111) = **7.1 calls**      |

Search-space illustration (tag D over measured N): the dataset's `httpx.*` package has **357** graph nodes. Uniform
search needs (357 + 1) / 2 ≈ 179 inspections in expectation. Four mechanism classes at f = 0.9 (S = 2.86x) would bring
that to about 63. The same classes read from issue text at the pilot's effective information (about 0.1 bits) would
save under 7%.

---

## G. Ready-to-Edit Whitepaper v3 Content (about 1,300 words)

*Placeholders such as `[M-E2: x%]` must be replaced by measured values with receipt IDs before submission.*

### G1. Abstract (draft, 170 words)

Small local models must localize bugs in unfamiliar repositories under tight time budgets. We study when
**classifying failures** speeds up that search. We model localization as guesswork over code locations and show that,
with K classes of fidelity f, expected search effort falls by 1/(1/K + 1 − f) and budgeted success rises by K·f. The
condition is that classes align with where faults live and the classifier **reorders rather than prunes**. On the 129
public tasks of the Gemma 4 Developer Agent Competition, issue text almost never contains a traceback (0/129), and
issue-text classes carry about 0.1 bits about the fix location. Class-conditioned priors therefore do not help. We then
measure **fault interference** in real reference patches by applying every subset of their hunks: `[M-E2: x%]` of
multi-hunk fixes are conjunctive, so progress is masked until all parts are present. We release the interference atlas
and task labels. We evaluate BCF-FC, an agent loop that probes, classifies the observed failure, orders fixes on a
dependency graph and re-probes after each fix. It resolves `[M-E4]` vs. `[M-E4]` tasks with a Gemma 4 31B QAT model.

### G2. §3 A search-theoretic model of localization (draft, 520 words)

Let Θ be the candidate code locations of a repository and L ∈ Θ the root cause with prior π. An agent that inspects
candidates in decreasing posterior order needs, in expectation, the guesswork $G(\pi)=\sum_i i p_{(i)}$ inspections.
Guesswork is exponential in uncertainty: $G \le 2^{H_{1/2}(\pi)}$ and $G \ge 2^{H_{1/2}(\pi)}/(1+\ln\lvert\Theta\rvert)$
(Arikan, 1996). Each bit of information about L therefore roughly halves the expected search.

A classifier maps observable evidence X to a class $\hat C$. Conditioning cannot increase optimal guesswork on
average, $G^*(L\mid\hat C)\le G^*(L)$, but its benefit is bounded by $I(L;\hat C)\le\min\{I(L;C), I(C;\hat C)\}\le\log_2 K$.
Three properties of a class system therefore matter: its **number** K (the ceiling), its **fidelity** (Fano's
inequality links error rate to lost information), and its **alignment** with code structure, $I(L;C)$.

In an idealized model with K equal regions and fidelity f, searching the predicted region first gives an expected
speed-up of $S(K,f)\approx 1/(1/K+1-f)$. Perfect fidelity yields S = K; any error caps the speed-up at 1/(1 − f)
regardless of K. Under a budget B no larger than one region, success probability rises by the factor K·f. Refining K to
K′ helps only if $1/K-1/K' > f-f'$. Pruning to the predicted region caps success at f; reordering keeps the speed-up at
no loss of coverage.

Estimation matters as much as design. With n training tasks, the plug-in estimate of $I(L;C)$ is inflated by about
$(\lvert\mathcal L\rvert-1)(K-1)/(2n\ln 2)$ bits. That is why we evaluate every class system with leave-one-out
guesswork and a within-repository permutation null.

Finally, a class can be **bought**: running a reproduction yields a failure signature (exception type, innermost
repository frame, assertion kind). Its value is the expected reduction in guesswork minus the probe's cost, the
minimum-entropy probing principle of model-based diagnosis. The Python exception hierarchy supplies a natural
coarse-to-fine class tree. The agent descends it only while the refinement condition holds.

### G3. §4 Masking, interference and chains (draft, 420 words)

Let $F_t(\Phi)$ indicate that test t fails when fault set Φ is present. A fault causes a failure only if the test
reaches it, it infects state, and the infection propagates to an assertion (the RIPR conditions). Fault a **masks**
fault b when a prevents b's reachability or propagation. Faults **interfere** whenever
$F_t(\{a,b\}) \ne F_t(\{a\}) \lor F_t(\{b\})$.

In real tasks we observe the dual: subsets of the reference fix. Splitting a patch into units $U$ and recording
$P_t(S)$ for every $S\subseteq U$, the Möbius transform
$\Delta_t(S)=\sum_{T\subseteq S}(-1)^{\lvert S\rvert-\lvert T\rvert}P_t(T)$ is zero for all multi-unit S iff the
fix is additive. A positive pairwise Δ marks **conjunctive** units: neither part shows progress alone. These are the
cases where an agent that edits, tests and backtracks receives no signal for its first correct change.

We model a task's faults as a DAG in which an edge $f_i\to f_j$ means $f_j$ is masked until $f_i$ is fixed. The correct
repair order is a topological order. If each attempt misattributes the next link to a downstream symptom with
probability $q_j$, the expected cost of a k-link chain is $kc+(c+r)\sum_j q_j/(1-q_j)$, with c the cost per attempt
and r the recovery cost. A method that lowers misattribution saves effort linearly in k when q is constant, and
superlinearly only when misattribution grows with depth. We test this condition rather than assume it. Because a guard
at a symptom site can make visible tests pass while hidden tests fail, BCF-FC re-probes after every fix. A new failure
signature is treated as an unmasking event, and the loop continues on the DAG.

### G4. §6.1 Pilot evidence (draft, 190 words)

Across the 129 public tasks, no problem statement contains a Python traceback and 15 name an exception type. An
issue-text classifier from our earlier design labels 95 tasks "unknown". Measured against a within-repository
permutation null, its classes carry at most about 0.12 bits about the fix file (fastapi, p = 0.08; rich and requests
not significant). Used as priors, they worsened leave-one-out localization: mean rank 6.57 vs. 5.84 and top-1 hits 23
vs. 30. Location priors also transfer poorly: 32% of fix files never occur in another task of the same repository.
These results rule out issue-text classes and file-level priors as the source of speed-up. They motivate classifying
**reproduced** failures and mapping classes to **search strategies**. Multi-part fixes are common: 55% of reference
patches have more than one hunk and 29% touch more than one source file. Hence the interference atlas in §5.

---

## H. How to Restate the Original Claim (suggested wording for the paper)

> *BCF localizes faults faster by classifying the failures it provokes and searching candidates in posterior order.
> In an idealized model, K classes of fidelity f speed up search by 1/(1/K + 1 − f) and multiply budgeted success by
> K·f. In practice the gain is bounded by the information the classes carry about fault location, which we measure.
> For multi-part faults, BCF orders fixes along a dependency graph and re-probes after each fix to detect masked
> faults.*

This version is falsifiable. It keeps the intuition ("number and fidelity"), names the missing third factor
(alignment, i.e. information), and ties every claim to a measurement in §E.

---

- Massey, J. L. (1994). Guessing and entropy. *Proc. IEEE ISIT*.
- Cover, T. M. & Thomas, J. A. (2006). *Elements of Information Theory*, 2nd ed. (data-processing and Fano inequalities).

# Task 2 (Separate Section) - Prior Research: Fault Interference, Fault Masking, Multi-Fault Repair, and Finding Interference-Prone Code

**Prepared:** 2026-09-30

**Sourcing note (important).** Your instructions limit research to the competition URL and files in this repo. None of
those files covers the fault-interference literature; the BCF compendium touches only agent-trajectory work. So this
section is compiled **from my background knowledge (training data through mid-2026), not from sources fetched in this
session**. I give each entry a confidence rating for its bibliographic details:

- **H** = high: well-known, details very likely right.
- **M** = medium: title, venue or year may be slightly off.

**Verify every citation against its primary source before it enters the paper.** Nothing here is marked as
measured.

---

## 1. Short Answer

1. **Fault interference and fault masking are established topics in software fault localization**, mainly in
   spectrum-based fault localization (SBFL), from about 2007 to 2020. Researchers seeded two or more faults into
   programs, usually C benchmarks such as the SIR/Siemens suite, and showed that faults change each other's pass/fail
   footprint:
   - **synergy:** more failing tests than either fault alone;
   - **obfuscation / masking:** fewer failing tests, or failures attributed to the wrong fault.

   Both make localization of at least one fault harder.
2. **The theory behind masking** comes from testing theory. The PIE/RIPR model says a fault must be reached, infect
   state, propagate and be revealed. Related phenomena are **coincidental correctness** (a faulty statement executes but
   the test passes) and **subtle higher-order mutants** (combined faults that hide each other).
3. **Multiple-fault diagnosis** has a Bayesian and information-theoretic tradition (GDE, BARINEL, entropy-based test
   prioritization). It matches the math in document 02: posterior over candidate diagnoses, minimum-entropy probing.
4. **Chained, multi-step issues** connect to cause-effect chain isolation (delta debugging), program slicing, and
   **multi-hunk program repair**. Patch-correctness research on overfitting explains why fixing the visible symptom can
   pass visible tests and fail hidden ones.
5. **LLM-agent research on GitHub issues** (SWE-bench line) focuses on localization, reproduction and patch selection.
   To my knowledge it has **not** measured interference or masking among the parts of real multi-hunk fixes, nor modelled
   agents' re-probing under masking. **That gap is the paper's opportunity** (verify with a targeted search before
   claiming it).
6. **Identifying where interference can occur** draws on program dependence graphs, dominator analysis,
   change-impact analysis and the theory of *non-interfering program versions*. It also draws on catalogues of
   error-hiding code (broad exception handlers, fallbacks, first-match logic). §6 turns this into a checklist that a
   stdlib-only skill script can implement.

---

## 2. Terminology (aligned with document 02)

| Term                            | Meaning                                                                                                        | Where it comes from                      |
| ------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- |
| Fault interference              | Two or more faults jointly produce test outcomes that differ from the combination of their individual outcomes | Multi-fault SBFL studies (§3.1)          |
| Fault synergy                   | Combination causes additional failures                                                                         | Multi-fault SBFL studies                 |
| Fault obfuscation / masking     | Combination hides failures, or makes them look caused by the other fault                                       | Multi-fault SBFL studies; testing theory |
| Coincidental correctness        | A fault executes (and may infect state) but the test still passes                                              | Testing / SBFL (§3.2)                    |
| RIPR / PIE                      | Reachability, Infection, Propagation, Revelation: conditions for a fault to cause an observable failure        | Testing theory (§3.2)                    |
| One-bug-at-a-time (OBA)         | Localize, fix and re-run repeatedly; the alternative is parallel debugging with failure clustering             | Multi-fault debugging (§3.1)             |
| Cause-effect chain              | The sequence of program states linking a defect to a failure                                                   | Delta debugging (§3.4)                   |
| Multi-hunk / multi-location fix | A correct patch that must change several code locations                                                        | Program repair (§3.5)                    |
| Overfitting (plausible) patch   | Passes the available tests but is not a correct fix                                                            | Patch-correctness studies (§3.5)         |

---

## 4. Which Parts Are Relevant to BCF, and How

| Prior research                                  | Use in BCF                                                                                                                                                                | Use in the paper                                                                             |
| ----------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Multi-fault SBFL and interference (§3.1)        | Definitions of synergy/masking; justification for a one-at-a-time loop with **re-probing** (at least one fault stays localizable)                                         | Related work; our atlas measures interference on *real* multi-hunk fixes, not seeded mutants |
| BARINEL, GDE, FLINT (§3.1, §3.3)                | Bayesian posterior over candidate locations and minimum-entropy probe choice, the formal basis of doc 02 §B7                                                              | Places the guesswork model inside the diagnosis tradition                                    |
| RIPR/PIE, coincidental correctness (§3.2)       | Masking = reachability or propagation failure; run the **whole affected test file**, not one test; distrust "passes now" after a guard edit                               | Theory section (doc 02 §C1)                                                                  |
| Higher-order mutants (§3.2)                     | **Generate chained-fault training and evaluation tasks**: combine two injected faults and keep pairs that mask each other (extends synthetic bug injection, plan item F5) | New resource candidate: masked-pair tasks                                                    |
| Delta debugging, slicing (§3.4)                 | ddmin over hunks for large patches (E6); callers/callees walk as a lightweight dynamic slice                                                                              | Method for the atlas at scale                                                                |
| Chianti change impact (§3.4)                    | Map each hunk to the tests it affects, which is the change-level analogue of the Möbius analysis                                                                          | Related work for the atlas                                                                   |
| Multi-hunk repair, evolutionary siblings (§3.5) | Prompt/skill rule: after fixing one site, **search for sibling sites** (same pattern elsewhere, sync/async twins)                                                         | Explains conjunctive atlas cases                                                             |
| Plausible vs. correct patches (§3.5)            | Rationale for the crash fork ("fix the state, not the crash site") and for hidden-test humility                                                                           | Threats-to-validity and motivation                                                           |
| LLM issue agents (§3.6)                         | Baselines and design references (Agentless reproduction; AutoCodeRover SBFL; SpecRover specs)                                                                             | Positioning: say plainly that phase structure and graph-first are not new                    |

### 4.1 Probable novelty gap (to confirm with a targeted literature search)

- Interference studies mostly used **seeded** faults in small **C or Java** programs, and measured the effect on
  **SBFL rankings**. I know of no study that measures **interaction structure among the hunks of real merged fixes**
  (Python, modern libraries) with a subset-enumeration / Möbius method. I also know of none that relates it to
  **LLM-agent** behaviour.
- LLM-agent papers report localization accuracy and resolve rates. They rarely model **masking-aware re-probing**, or
  the **information content** of issue text vs. reproduced failures.
- The competition's 129 tasks + hidden-test harness make both measurable at low cost (doc 02 §E). **Claim novelty only
  after a search of 2024-2026 venues** (ICSE, FSE, ISSTA, ASE, ICLR, NeurIPS, ACL) and arXiv.

---

## 5. Identifying Scenarios Where Fault Interference Can Occur

### 5.1 Foundations

| Idea                                                                             | What it lets you detect                                                                                       | Conf. |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----- |
| Program dependence graph (Ferrante, Ottenstein, Warren, ACM TOPLAS 1987)         | Where two code sites' effects converge (shared data or control dependences), which makes interaction possible | H     |
| Dominator trees in control-flow graphs (Lengauer, Tarjan, ACM TOPLAS 1979)       | If site a dominates site b, a fault at a can block b's reachability (reachability masking)                    | H     |
| Non-interfering program versions (Horwitz, Prins, Reps, ACM TOPLAS 1989)         | Formal test of whether two changes interfere, via slices; the static analogue of the atlas                    | H     |
| Combinatorial interaction faults (Kuhn, Wallace, Gallo, IEEE TSE 2004)           | Empirically, most failures are triggered by interactions of a few factors; a prior on chain length            | H     |
| Code Property Graphs (Yamaguchi et al., IEEE S&P 2014; already cited in draft 2) | A unified AST + CFG + PDG representation suited to such queries                                               | H     |

The released competition graphs contain only `calls` edges (measured). Data-flow and dominance must therefore be
computed at runtime from source with Python's `ast` module, or offline for dataset analysis.

### 5.2 Practical signal catalogue (implementable as a stdlib skill script `interference_scan.py`)

| Code pattern (Python)                                                                        | RIPR mechanism                      | Interference type                                                        | Detection                                                                                                     | BCF action                                                                                 |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Early `return` / `raise` / `continue` guards on shared paths                                 | Reachability                        | Masking of downstream faults                                             | AST: guard statements before call sites; dominance within a function                                          | After fixing an upstream guard, re-probe for newly reachable failures                      |
| Broad handlers: bare `except`, `except Exception`, `contextlib.suppress`, `try/except: pass` | Propagation                         | Masking; failure surfaces far away                                       | AST scan of handler types and bodies                                                                          | Treat swallowed-error sites as candidate origins when the symptom is "silent wrong output" |
| Fallback defaults: `dict.get(k, d)`, `x or default`, `getattr(o, a, None)`                   | Propagation (infection absorbed)    | Masking; cancellation                                                    | AST patterns                                                                                                  | Check the producer of the value, not only the consumer                                     |
| State reset or re-initialization in loops (`buf = []` inside a loop)                         | Infection                           | Masking of downstream consumers (the hypothetical httpx history pattern) | AST: assignment of an empty container inside a loop that also appends                                         | Classic "state accumulation" class                                                         |
| First-match / order-dependent logic: `[0]`, `next(...)`, first declaration wins              | Infection                           | Order-dependent faults; symptoms depend on input order                   | AST: subscript `[0]` or `next()` over collections built from user declarations                                | Probe with reordered inputs                                                                |
| Single-use resources: generators, streams, iterators consumed once                           | Infection → delayed crash           | Crash downstream of the real cause                                       | AST: generator functions, `.read()`/iteration of the same object twice                                        | Crash fork: prefer "fix the state"                                                         |
| Sync/async twins or duplicated code paths                                                    | Reachability (only one path tested) | Conjunctive fixes (both copies needed), as in evolutionary siblings      | Graph: parallel `httpx.*` / `ahttpx.*` packages in the dataset (357 and 239 nodes, measured); AST clone check | After a fix, search for the twin and apply the same change                                 |
| Shared mutable state: module globals, caches (`lru_cache`), class attributes                 | Infection spreads across calls      | Synergy; test-order dependence                                           | AST: module-level mutable assignments, decorators                                                             | Run the whole test file; watch for order effects                                           |
| Wrapper, middleware or decorator chains                                                      | Propagation through layers          | Symptom at the outer layer, cause inside                                 | Graph: long `calls` chains; AST: decorators                                                                   | Walk callees of the symptom site                                                           |
| Multiple assertions in one test (pytest stops at the first failing assert)                   | Revelation                          | **Assertion masking**: later checks invisible until earlier ones pass    | AST of the test (reproduction scripts only; hidden tests are unseen)                                          | Write reproductions with independent checks, or `pytest -x` per case                       |
| Normalization pairs (encode/decode, to_str/parse)                                            | Infection                           | Cancellation: two wrongs make a right, and fixing one exposes the other  | AST: paired conversion calls                                                                                  | Expect a second failure after the first fix; re-probe                                      |

### 5.3 How to measure these scenarios (links to document 02)

1. **Static prevalence:** run `interference_scan.py` over all 129 snapshots, and count patterns in the files touched by
   reference fixes vs. random files. This tests whether interference-prone patterns are enriched at fix sites.
2. **Dynamic confirmation:** the interference atlas (doc 02 §E2) labels tasks as conjunctive or antagonistic. Correlate
   the labels with static patterns at the hunk sites (odds ratios with exact CIs).
3. **Agent relevance:** on atlas-conjunctive tasks, compare misattribution q and cost for baseline vs. BCF-FC
   (doc 02 §E5).

---

## 6. Recommended Related-Work Paragraph Plan for the Paper (about 300 words)

| Sentence group                          | Cite (after verification)                                                                     |
| --------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Multi-fault localization & interference | Jones et al. 2007; DiGiuseppe & Jones 2011; Debroy & Wong 2009; Abreu et al. 2009             |
| Masking theory                          | Voas 1992; Ammann & Offutt 2016; Wang et al. 2009; Jia & Harman 2009                          |
| Diagnosis & information                 | de Kleer & Williams 1987; Yoo et al. 2013; Arikan 1996                                        |
| Chains & multi-hunk repair              | Zeller 2002; Saha et al. 2019; Sobreira et al. 2018                                           |
| Plausible patches                       | Qi et al. 2015; Smith et al. 2015                                                             |
| LLM issue agents                        | Jimenez et al. 2024; Xia et al. 2024; Zhang et al. 2024; Ruan et al. 2024; Ouyang et al. 2025 |

### 6.1 Verification checklist before citing

- [ ] Confirm authors, title, venue and year of every entry marked M from its primary source (publisher page, DBLP or arXiv)
- [ ] Confirm the synergy/obfuscation terminology in DiGiuseppe & Jones (ICSM 2011) before using the exact words
- [ ] Run a targeted search (2024-2026) for "multi-hunk interference", "fault masking LLM agent", "hunk dependency
  SWE-bench", and similar, before stating the novelty gap in §4.1
- [ ] Prefer peer-reviewed versions over arXiv where both exist


---
